    "content": "Previous Candidates:\n[\"Binge-Eating Disorder\"]\n\nInterview Transcript:\n\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see us today?\n\nPatient: I'm really embarrassed to admit this, but I've been struggling a lot with eating massive amounts of food in one sitting. Once I start, I feel completely unable to stop or control myself. It's getting really distressing, and I just don't know what to do.\n\nDoctor: After these episodes of eating large amounts of food, do you ever try to compensate by vomiting, using laxatives, or exercising excessively to get rid of the calories?\n\nPatient: No, I don't do any of those things. I've never forced myself to throw up, used laxatives, or tried to exercise excessively to burn off the food. I usually just end up sitting there feeling incredibly guilty and miserable afterward.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating large amounts of food occur, and have you noticed any pattern to when they happen?\n\nPatient: They've been happening on average at least once a week, and this has been going on for about seven months now. I haven't really noticed any specific pattern or timing for when they happen, though.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about the feelings of guilt and misery you experience after these episodes, and how they affect your daily life and relationships?\n\nPatient: To be honest, I'm not really sure how to explain it, and I haven't thought much about how it impacts my daily life or relationships. I mostly just try to block it out and get through the day after it happens.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes of eating, do you feel like you're eating much more quickly than usual, or eating until you're uncomfortably full, and is it hard for you to stop eating even when you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes, during those times I definitely eat much faster than usual, and I don't stop until I feel painfully and uncomfortably full. It really feels like I have absolutely no control, and stopping once I've started feels completely impossible.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself eating these large amounts of food alone, or hiding your eating from others due to feelings of shame or embarrassment?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about that, to be honest. I haven't really noticed if I specifically try to eat alone or hide my food from others.\n\nDoctor: Can you think back to a recent episode of binge eating and describe it to me in more detail, such as what triggered it, what you ate, and how you felt during and after the episode?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure what triggered it, but the other night I started eating massive amounts of whatever food I could find, shoving it down incredibly fast. I felt a complete loss of control and just couldn't stop myself until I was painfully, uncomfortably full. Afterward, I just felt physically miserable and incredibly guilty about what I had done.\n\nDoctor: How would you describe your relationship with food and eating when you're not having one of these binge episodes - do you restrict your food intake or follow a very strict diet, and do you think about food or your weight frequently?\n\nPatient: Outside of those episodes, I don't really restrict my food or follow any kind of strict diet. I'm not really sure if I think about my weight or food too much otherwise, as my eating feels pretty normal when I'm not having an episode.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods where you were dieting or trying to lose weight, and if so, did these periods coincide with the start of your binge eating episodes or make them worse?\n\nPatient: I haven't really tried dieting or actively trying to lose weight, so I'm not sure if there is any connection. I don't think dieting had anything to do with why or when these eating episodes started."

{"candidates": ["Binge-Eating Disorder"], "note": "The patient's symptoms and experiences, as described in the interview, continue to align closely with the criteria for Binge-Eating Disorder. The patient reports recurrent episodes of eating large amounts of food in a short period, feeling a lack of control during these episodes, and experiencing distress afterward. The absence of compensatory behaviors (like vomiting, laxatives, or excessive exercise) to counteract the binge eating, as seen in other eating disorders, further supports this diagnosis. The frequency and duration of these episodes (at least once a week for the past seven months) meet the diagnostic criteria. The patient's description of eating rapidly, to the point of discomfort, and the feelings of guilt and misery post-episode are also characteristic of Binge-Eating Disorder. Since the patient's description does not indicate any significant periods of dieting or restrictive eating that could suggest other eating disorders like Bulimia Nervosa, and there's no mention of psychotic symptoms, mood episodes, or other criteria that would suggest a different psychiatric condition from the allowed list, Binge-Eating Disorder remains the most fitting diagnosis based on the information provided.", "is_final": true}

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.
